Smithfield Foods, Inc. and subsidiaries Smithfield Packing Co. and Gwaltney of Smithfield (Smithfield, Virginia slaughterhouse and meat-processing complex) (1991-1997)Industrial Water
Section 402 (NPDES permit) and Section 309 (civil penalties); violations of effluent and monitoring/reporting requirements under 33 U.S.C. 1311, 1318, 1342
Violation

Smithfield's slaughterhouse and on-site wastewater plant repeatedly exceeded NPDES discharge limits for phosphorus, ammonia, cyanide, oil and grease, fecal coliform, and total Kjeldahl nitrogen into the Pagan River (a Chesapeake Bay tributary). The court found nearly 7,000 separate Clean Water Act violations between 1991 and 1997, including chronic limit exceedances, falsified Discharge Monitoring Reports, and a 5-year delay in installing required pollution-control upgrades.

Outcome

U.S. District Court (E.D. Va.) imposed a $12.6 million civil penalty in August 1997 — the largest CWA civil penalty in history at the time — affirmed by the Fourth Circuit in 1999. Required full upgrade of on-site wastewater treatment and tie-in to the Hampton Roads Sanitation District.

Relevance to data centers

Establishes that chronic NPDES-limit exceedances and inaccurate Discharge Monitoring Reports trigger per-day, per-parameter civil penalties that can reach into the eight figures. Data centers self-reporting cooling-tower-blowdown DMRs face the same per-violation penalty exposure under 33 U.S.C. 1319(d).

Sources
DOJ Press Release #331: Smithfield Foods Fined $12.6 Million, Largest Clean Water Act Fine Ever (DOJ press release) · EPA Newsroom: Appeals Court Upholds Ruling Against Smithfield Foods for Polluting Virginia River (EPA press release) · Bay Journal: Smithfield gets record fine for polluting Virginia river (news)
City and County of San Francisco v. Environmental Protection Agency, 604 U.S. 334 (2025)Landmark Precedent
CWA Section 301(b)(1)(C) / 33 U.S.C. § 1311(b)(1)(C) (NPDES permit conditions)
Violation

San Francisco challenged its renewed combined-sewer NPDES permit for the Oceanside facility, which contained two 'end-result' provisions: a prohibition on any discharge that would 'contribute to a violation' of receiving-water quality standards and a prohibition on discharges creating 'pollution, contamination, or nuisance' under state law. The city argued EPA had to specify the exact effluent limits required for compliance rather than make the permittee bear the risk of receiving-water exceedances it does not control.

Outcome

Decided March 4, 2025, by 5-4. Justice Alito wrote the majority opinion, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and Justices Thomas, Gorsuch, and Kavanaugh, holding that § 1311(b)(1)(C) does not authorize EPA to impose generic 'end-result' provisions making permittees liable for downstream water-quality violations without telling them what specific effluent limits to meet. Justice Barrett dissented in relevant part, joined by Justices Sotomayor, Kagan, and Jackson, agreeing the broader narrative arguments failed but contending end-result conditions are lawful under the statute.

Relevance to data centers

Data centers whose cooling-tower blowdown or stormwater discharges run through municipal POTWs or hold direct NPDES permits can no longer be hit with open-ended 'don't violate water-quality standards' language; EPA and delegated state agencies must instead set numeric or technology-based effluent limits, increasing permit predictability but shifting the regulatory burden back to permit writers.

Sources
City and County of San Francisco v. EPA, 604 U.S. 334 (2025) — Justia (court ruling) · Oyez — City and County of San Francisco v. EPA (No. 23-753) (court ruling) · Sidley — SCOTUS Limits EPA's CWA Authority to Impose Water Quality Standards (Mar. 2025) (law review)
Puget Soundkeeper Alliance v. Port of Tacoma, No. 21-35881 (9th Cir. 2024); cert. denied sub nom. Port of Tacoma v. Puget Soundkeeper Alliance, No. 24-350 (U.S. June 30, 2025). (2024-2025)Landmark Precedent
CWA Section 505 citizen-suit scope and its interaction with the permit shield — whether citizens may enforce, in federal court, state-law NPDES permit conditions that are broader or stricter than the federal CWA floor.
Violation

The Ninth Circuit held that CWA citizen suits can enforce the entirety of a state-issued NPDES permit, including conditions exceeding federal minimums (here, Washington's Industrial Stormwater General Permit). The U.S. Solicitor General filed an amicus brief urging the Supreme Court to grant review and reverse, arguing the ruling 'contravenes the CWA's text, structure, and context.'

Outcome

The Supreme Court denied certiorari on June 30, 2025, leaving the Ninth Circuit ruling intact. The Fourth and Eleventh Circuits align with it; the Second Circuit disagrees, so the circuit split — and non-uniform citizen-suit exposure nationally — persists.

Relevance to data centers

Confirms environmental groups can sue in federal court to enforce state NPDES permit terms stricter than federal law — directly raising citizen-suit exposure for the receiving WWTPs whose state-issued permits this pipeline already tracks, and for any facility operating under a state discharge permit.

Sources
Supreme Court Declines to Hear Cases Seeking to Narrow Citizen Suit Enforcement, Leaving Doors Wide Open (law firm analysis) · Clean Water Act Citizen Suits in the Spotlight: Solicitor General Calls for Supreme Court Review (law firm analysis) · Supreme Court's Cert Denials Pave Way for Surge in Environmental Citizen Suits (bar association article)
Coastal Environmental Rights Foundation v. Naples Restaurant Group, LLC, No. 23-55469 (9th Cir.); opinion filed Sept. 18, 2024, amended Nov. 5, 2025. (2024-2025)Landmark Precedent
CWA Section 505 citizen-suit mootness and the survival of civil-penalty claims — whether a penalty claim keeps a citizen suit alive after the discharger comes into permit compliance.
Violation

A nonprofit sued over unpermitted Fourth-of-July fireworks discharges into Alamitos Bay (Long Beach, CA). After the defendant obtained an NPDES permit authorizing the discharge, the Ninth Circuit held the suit constitutionally moot — including the civil-penalty claim — because it was 'absolutely clear that the allegedly wrongful behavior could not reasonably be expected to recur,' so penalties lost their deterrent function (applying Friends of the Earth v. Laidlaw).

Outcome

Suit dismissed as moot. The Ninth Circuit aligned with the Eighth Circuit; the Second, Third, Fourth, Seventh, and Eleventh Circuits hold that civil-penalty claims survive mootness — deepening the circuit split.

Relevance to data centers

Narrows when CWA citizen suits can proceed: once a discharger obtains a permit or otherwise cures a violation, the Ninth Circuit treats penalty-only claims as moot — a permit-shield-style defense a data center or its receiving WWTP could invoke after coming into compliance, blunting citizen-suit deterrence.

Sources
Coastal Environmental Rights Foundation v. Naples Restaurant Group, LLC — opinion (9th Cir.) (court opinion) · Coastal Environmental Rights Foundation v. Naples Restaurant Group, LLC (9th Cir. 2024) (court opinion (Justia)) · Ninth Circuit Finds Clean Water Act Suit Seeking Only Civil Penalties Becomes Moot Once Wrongful Conduct Ceased (litigation blog analysis)
Loper Bright Enterprises et al. v. Raimondo, 603 U.S. 369 (2024), No. 22-451 (consolidated with Relentless, Inc. v. Department of Commerce, No. 22-1219)Landmark Precedent
Administrative Procedure Act § 706 review of all federal agency action — overrules Chevron v. NRDC (1984) and Auer v. Robbins (1997) deference. Directly reshapes how every CWA NPDES permitting decision, effluent guideline, and 'waters of the United States' interpretation by EPA is reviewed in court.
Violation

Atlantic herring fishermen challenged a National Marine Fisheries Service rule requiring them to pay for on-board federal monitors. The case was used as a vehicle to challenge the Chevron doctrine — the 40-year-old rule that courts defer to reasonable agency interpretations of ambiguous statutes. The Supreme Court held that the Administrative Procedure Act requires courts reviewing agency action to exercise independent judgment on questions of law and that agency interpretations are entitled only to Skidmore-style respect (persuasive weight), not binding deference.

Outcome

6-3 decision (Roberts for majority; Sotomayor, Kagan, Jackson dissenting), June 28, 2024. Chevron deference overruled. The decision was applied within months by the Supreme Court in City and County of San Francisco v. EPA (March 2025) to strike down EPA's use of end-result NPDES limits, demonstrating its real-world bite on CWA implementation.

Relevance to data centers

Direct relevance to data-center water tracking: every EPA interpretation of Section 402 (NPDES), Section 401 (state certification), Section 404 (dredge-and-fill), and the post-Sackett WOTUS rule is now reviewable de novo. EPA cannot rely on agency-deference to defend novel cooling-water effluent guidelines or technology-based limits for data centers — any future rulemaking targeting hyperscale cooling water (Ohio's draft OHD000001, Virginia's data-center reporting bills, EPA's MSGP 2026) faces a heightened judicial-review standard.

Sources
Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo, 603 U.S. 369 (2024) — Supreme Court Opinion (PDF) (court ruling) · Environmental Law Implications of Loper Bright and the End of Chevron Deference — Sidley (law firm analysis) · U.S. Supreme Court Overturns Chevron Deference — WateReuse Association (trade association analysis) · After Loper Bright: Weakening the Power of the Environmental Protection Agency — JHULR (law review)
Sackett v. Environmental Protection Agency, 598 U.S. 651 (2023)Landmark Precedent
Section 502(7) (definition of 'waters of the United States') and Section 404 (wetlands dredge-and-fill permits)
Violation

EPA ordered the Sacketts to stop backfilling their Idaho lot, classifying it as a federally regulated wetland with a 'significant nexus' to a nearby creek-and-lake system. The Sacketts sued, arguing the wetland lacked any continuous surface connection to navigable waters and therefore was not a 'water of the United States' subject to CWA jurisdiction.

Outcome

Unanimous in judgment (9-0 reversing the Ninth Circuit) but 5-4 on the controlling test. Justice Alito's majority opinion rejected Justice Kennedy's Rapanos 'significant nexus' standard and held that the CWA reaches only (1) relatively permanent bodies of water 'connected to traditional interstate navigable waters' and (2) wetlands with a 'continuous surface connection' to those waters such that they are 'indistinguishable' from them.

Relevance to data centers

Sackett dramatically shrank federal CWA jurisdiction over wetlands, ephemeral streams, and isolated waters — the exact categories most likely to receive stormwater runoff or graywater seepage from a large data center campus. After Sackett, many data center sites that previously needed §402 NPDES or §404 permits for site drainage now fall to state-only regulation, shifting the compliance burden to state programs in Virginia and Ohio.

Sources
Sackett v. EPA, 598 U.S. 651 (2023) — Supreme Court slip opinion (court ruling) · Sackett Decision Provides Clarity, Substantially Restricts Clean Water Act Jurisdiction Scope (Holland & Knight) (law review) · Supreme Court Narrows Federal Jurisdiction Under Clean Water Act (Congressional Research Service) (law review)
Sierra Club v. West Virginia Department of Environmental Protection, 64 F.4th 487 (4th Cir. 2023) (2023)Landmark Precedent
CWA Section 401 / 33 U.S.C. § 1341 (state water quality certification)
Violation

Sierra Club and other environmental groups petitioned for review of West Virginia DEP's Section 401 water-quality certification authorizing Mountain Valley Pipeline to make hundreds of stream crossings in the state. Petitioners argued the certification was arbitrary because the agency failed to account for MVP's history of erosion and sedimentation violations and did not impose stream-specific conditions or anti-degradation analysis.

Outcome

On April 3, 2023, a unanimous Fourth Circuit panel (Judges Wynn, Thacker, and Heytens) vacated the WVDEP § 401 certification. The court held the agency's 'reasonable assurance' determination was arbitrary and capricious because it (1) failed to address MVP's violation history, (2) did not incorporate stormwater-permit and SWPPP conditions, (3) provided no reasoned basis for relying on EPA's upland construction general permit, and (4) skipped location-specific antidegradation review.

Relevance to data centers

For data-center developers whose campuses involve federally permitted stream or wetland crossings (e.g., long-haul fiber, water-supply pipelines, electrical transmission corridors), state § 401 certifications must rest on documented project-specific anti-degradation review and explicit stormwater conditions — a boilerplate 'reasonable assurance' memo is now legally vulnerable in the Fourth Circuit, which covers Virginia.

Sources
Sierra Club v. West Virginia DEP, No. 22-1008 (4th Cir. Apr. 3, 2023) (court ruling) · Fourth Circuit Opinion No. 22-1008 (PDF) (court ruling) · Appalachian Voices — Court vacates critical West Virginia water permit for MVP (law review)
Garry L. Lewis and G. Lewis-Louisiana, L.L.C. v. United States, 88 F.4th 1073 (5th Cir. 2023), No. 21-30163 (2023)Landmark Precedent
CWA Section 404 (dredge-and-fill permits) / 33 U.S.C. § 1344; 'waters of the United States' jurisdiction under 33 U.S.C. § 1362(7)
Violation

Louisiana timber-plantation owner Garry Lewis requested a jurisdictional determination from the Army Corps of Engineers in 2013 to develop property used as a pine timber plantation. The Corps concluded in 2016 that portions of the property contained wetlands subject to CWA jurisdiction under the post-Rapanos significant-nexus test. Lewis filed suit; the case took ten years through the district court and the Fifth Circuit.

Outcome

On December 18, 2023, the Fifth Circuit (panel of Judges Edith H. Jones, Stuart Kyle Duncan, and Jacques L. Wiener) unanimously applied the Supreme Court's May 2023 Sackett decision and held that the Lewis property was not within CWA jurisdiction. The court refused to remand to the Corps for further proceedings, instead directing the district court to enter judgment that the property does not contain WOTUS. In a striking footnote, Judges Jones and Duncan warned that further Corps attempts to assert jurisdiction over Lewis's property could be sanctioned under the Equal Access to Justice Act's bad-faith provision.

Relevance to data centers

First federal appellate application of Sackett to a real-world Section 404 wetlands jurisdictional dispute. The Fifth Circuit's refusal to remand sets a precedent that lower courts can apply Sackett's continuous-surface-connection test directly without Corps deference — accelerating the practical contraction of CWA jurisdiction. For data center sites that were previously subject to Section 404 wetlands mitigation (a routine cost item for Northern Virginia and Loudoun County sites), Lewis confirms the post-Sackett framework where many isolated and headwater wetlands now fall outside CWA jurisdiction and outside developer mitigation obligations.

Sources
Lewis v. USA, No. 21-30163 (5th Cir. 2023) — Justia (court ruling) · United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit — Opinion 21-30163-CV0 (PDF) (court ruling) · Fifth Circuit Applies U.S. Supreme Court's Sackett Decision to End Long-Standing WOTUS Dispute — Energy Law Blog (law firm analysis) · An early Christmas present from three Fifth Circuit Judges who concluded a Louisiana property is not subject to Federal Clean Water Act Jurisdiction — ACOEL (law review)
Hawai'i Wildlife Fund v. County of Maui, 550 F. Supp. 3d 871 (D. Haw. 2021) (on remand from 590 U.S. 165) (2021)Landmark Precedent
CWA Section 402 / 33 U.S.C. § 1342 (NPDES) and § 502(12) definition of 'discharge of a pollutant'
Violation

On remand from the Supreme Court's 2020 Maui ruling, the District of Hawai'i had to apply the new 'functional equivalent of a direct discharge' test to the County of Maui's Lahaina Wastewater Reclamation Facility, which injected approximately 3-5 MGD of treated effluent into deep wells from which pollutants migrated through groundwater to the Pacific Ocean. A tracer-dye study showed transit times as short as 84 days with an average of 14-16 months over distances under 1.5 miles.

Outcome

On July 26, 2021, the district court (Mollway, J.) held the injections were the functional equivalent of a direct discharge and that the County therefore required an NPDES permit. The court emphasized the short distance, modest transit time, and intact chemical character of pollutants on arrival as decisive Maui factors, rejecting the County's argument that groundwater attenuation rendered the discharge non-jurisdictional.

Relevance to data centers

Data centers that inject cooling-tower blowdown into the subsurface, rely on percolation ponds, or send PFAS-laden effluent to land-application systems near surface water cannot assume groundwater conveyance immunizes them from NPDES requirements; short distances and traceable pollutants will trigger Maui's functional-equivalent test.

Sources
Hawai'i Wildlife Fund v. County of Maui, ECF No. 468 (D. Haw. July 26, 2021) (court ruling) · County of Maui v. Hawaii Wildlife Fund, 590 U.S. 165 (2020) — Justia (court ruling) · Center for Agricultural Law and Taxation — Federal Court Says Maui 'Effectively Discharging' Pollutant (law review)
County of Maui v. Hawaii Wildlife Fund, 590 U.S. 165 (2020)Landmark Precedent
Section 402 (NPDES permit requirement) and Section 502(12) (definition of 'discharge of a pollutant' from a point source)
Violation

Maui County's Lahaina wastewater facility injected 3-5 million gallons per day of treated effluent into Class V underground wells. Tracer studies showed roughly 90% of that water migrated through groundwater to the Pacific Ocean within months. Environmental groups argued the county needed an NPDES permit; the county argued groundwater conveyance breaks the §402 'from a point source' chain.

Outcome

6-3 decision (Breyer, joined by Roberts, Ginsburg, Sotomayor, Kagan, Kavanaugh). The Court held that an NPDES permit is required when a discharge through groundwater is 'the functional equivalent of a direct discharge' into navigable waters, with time and distance the most important of seven listed factors. Thomas, Gorsuch, and Alito dissented.

Relevance to data centers

Directly relevant to data centers that send cooling tower blowdown, evaporative bleed, or treated process water to on-site leach fields, injection wells, or lined-but-permeable ponds rather than to a municipal sewer. If that water reaches a navigable water in a short distance/time, Maui says a federal §402 permit is required even with no pipe to the surface — a live issue for desert/inland Virginia and Ohio sites.

Sources
County of Maui v. Hawaii Wildlife Fund, 590 U.S. 165 (2020) — Supreme Court slip opinion (court ruling) · County of Maui v. Hawaii Wildlife Fund (Harvard Law Review case comment) (law review) · County of Maui v. Hawaii Wildlife Fund (Wikipedia) (news)
Entergy Corp. v. Riverkeeper, Inc., 556 U.S. 208 (2009)Landmark Precedent
Section 316(b) (cooling water intake structures — 'best technology available for minimizing adverse environmental impact')
Violation

EPA's 2004 Phase II rule governed cooling water intakes at ~500 existing power plants and allowed cost-benefit analysis when setting impingement/entrainment performance standards and site-specific variances. Riverkeeper and other environmental groups argued §316(b)'s 'best technology available' language forbids EPA from weighing dollar costs against ecological benefits.

Outcome

5-4 decision (Scalia for the majority, joined by Roberts, Kennedy, Thomas, Alito; Stevens dissenting with Souter and Ginsburg; Breyer concurring in part). Held that '[i]t was well within the bounds of reasonable interpretation for the EPA to conclude that cost-benefit analysis is not categorically forbidden' under §316(b), reversing the Second Circuit and largely upholding the Phase II rule.

Relevance to data centers

Most data centers don't trigger §316(b) directly — it applies to intakes designed to withdraw more than 2 MGD from waters of the US for cooling, and the vast majority of data centers buy potable water from municipal utilities. But Entergy's broader cost-benefit doctrine carries over to EPA standard-setting under other CWA provisions, including any future effluent guidelines or §304(m) rules aimed at data center cooling discharges or PFAS-bearing immersion-cooling fluids.

Sources
Entergy Corp. v. Riverkeeper, Inc. — Cornell LII syllabus (court ruling) · Entergy Corp. v. Riverkeeper Inc. (Wikipedia) (news) · DOJ ENRD case summary — Entergy Corp. v. Riverkeeper (court ruling)
Rapanos v. United States, 547 U.S. 715 (2006)Landmark Precedent
Section 502(7) (definition of 'waters of the United States') and Section 404 (wetlands dredge-and-fill permits)
Violation

John Rapanos filled roughly 22 acres of Michigan wetlands without a §404 permit to develop a shopping mall; the wetlands sat near man-made drains that eventually fed traditional navigable waters. The companion Carabell case involved wetlands separated from a tributary by a man-made berm. The question was whether such hydrologically connected but physically separate wetlands counted as 'waters of the United States.'

Outcome

4-1-4 fractured ruling vacating and remanding. Justice Scalia's plurality (with Roberts, Thomas, Alito) required 'relatively permanent' waters plus a 'continuous surface connection' to navigable waters. Justice Kennedy's controlling concurrence required only a 'significant nexus' — that the wetland 'significantly affects the physical, biological, and chemical integrity' of downstream navigable waters. No single test commanded a majority, producing 17 years of regulatory whiplash.

Relevance to data centers

Rapanos is the doctrinal precursor to Sackett: lower courts and EPA generally applied Kennedy's broader 'significant nexus' test, which kept a wide universe of wetlands and intermittent streams within federal jurisdiction. For data centers, Rapanos meant that ditches, retention ponds, and intermittent streams crossing campus sites were often federally regulated — a regime now superseded by Sackett's narrower surface-connection rule.

Sources
Rapanos v. United States, 547 U.S. 715 (2006) — FindLaw (court ruling) · EPA — Rapanos v. United States & Carabell v. United States summary (court ruling) · Rapanos v. United States (Wikipedia) (news)

Part 2 — Active & Potential CWA Exposure at Named Data Center Sites

12 named data center sites where regulatory proceedings are active (pending permit applications, ongoing investigations, active citizen suits) or where the factual circumstances match the historical enforcement patterns above — but no formal CWA enforcement action has been issued yet. Use the theories panel above to trace which CWA hook applies to each site.

Meta Platforms Inc., Stanton Springs / Newton-Morgan County, Georgia data center campus (2026)Data Center
Not yet formally cited. Potential CWA Section 402 (construction stormwater) and Safe Drinking Water Act review; EPA Office of Water has only pledged investigation, not issued an enforcement action.
Violation

Residents of Morgan County, Georgia adjacent to Meta's Stanton Springs data center report well water turning brown and turbid following blasting and clearing for data center construction; appliances failing; families reliant on bottled water. Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez presented jars of discolored water at a congressional hearing in May 2026. EPA Assistant Administrator for Water Jessica Kramer publicly committed at that hearing to review whether the construction project has affected drinking water quality.

Outcome

PENDING — no formal CWA enforcement action, NOV, or consent order issued as of 2026-05-26. EPA review announced but not concluded. Meta commissioned its own independent groundwater study claiming no operational impact. No Georgia EPD enforcement action publicly documented.

Relevance to data centers

Highest-profile potential CWA enforcement matter against a hyperscaler currently active, but at this point it is a pledged federal investigation, not an enforcement action. Distinguishing this from a closed case matters: as of late May 2026, no hyperscaler has been formally cited under CWA in this matter. The case illustrates both the regulatory attention now focused on data centers and the lag between resident complaints and formal agency action.

Sources
EPA Official Agrees to Review Data Center Impacts on Water (news) · AOC presses EPA over Morgan County drinking water concerns tied to Georgia data center development (news) · Meta data center allegedly muddies Georgia town's drinking water, investigation underway (news)
Nscale / Fidelis New Energy — Monarch Compute Campus, University Lane, Mason County, West Virginia (Meadowlands Estates adjacent) (2026)Data Center
Potential CWA Section 402 (NPDES construction stormwater) — formal enforcement under West Virginia DEP authority pending. As of May 25, 2026 no NOV had been issued; a WV DEP inspection the week prior had found the site in compliance.
Violation

On May 23-24, 2026, severe flooding hit Meadowlands Estates after a silt-fence section in the construction stormwater retention area at the Monarch/Nscale data center site failed during a rain event delivering roughly a month's rainfall in 48 hours under a Flash Flood Warning. Nscale acknowledged the storm 'exceeded the design capacity' of the temporary erosion controls. Civil reimbursement, not federal enforcement, is the primary current track.

Outcome

PENDING as of May 26, 2026 — No formal CWA NOV or consent order issued. Nscale committed to cover all cleanup/repair costs, hotel rooms, and meals for affected residents. West Virginia National Guard and state officials were on-site coordinating evaluation; WV DEP discussing corrective and prevention plans. No filed civil suit or class action yet reported.

Relevance to data centers

The most recent and most visible 'construction stormwater fails catastrophically' incident in the data center sector. Even where the site was 'permit-compliant' the week before, AI-era buildout pace plus climate-intensified rainfall produced real-world property damage — exactly the failure mode that hardens citizen-suit pipelines and fuels stricter state CGP terms.

Sources
Severe flooding in Mason County relating to data center construction — WSAZ (news) · Some Mason County residents experience flooding near site of Nscale development — Charleston Gazette-Mail (news) · Mason County community impacted by flooding from data center construction site — WV MetroNews (news)
AMES Construction (general contractor for Beale Infrastructure / Project Blue hyperscale data center, South Houghton Road, Pima County, Arizona — believed to be an Amazon Web Services site, though Beale has not confirmed end-user) (2026)Data Center
Pima County DEQ fugitive-dust permit (Arizona implementation of CAA), tied directly to the project's water-supply dispute — City of Tucson revoked AMES's hydrant meter permit in May 2026, triggering construction water-use changes that caused the dust violation. Not a direct CWA NPDES action, but illustrates the water-permit / CWA-stormwater crossover at hyperscale sites.
Violation

On May 8 and May 11, 2026, Pima County DEQ inspectors documented excess fugitive dust crossing property boundary lines from the Project Blue construction site, with no dust-control measures in use. Pima County issued a Notice of Violation to AMES Construction on or about May 13, 2026, with potential fines of up to $10,000 per day per violation under Arizona state law. Root cause: the City of Tucson's May 2026 revocation of AMES's water-meter hydrant permit (after the city declared Project Blue ineligible for any municipal resources, including water), forcing the contractor to halt dust-suppression water trucking before establishing an alternative non-potable source. Separately, ADWR approved well-drilling permits for Bobcat Tucson Water LLC (a Beale Infrastructure SPV) for two on-site wells authorized to withdraw up to 96.5 acre-feet (~31.4 million gallons) per year.

Outcome

PENDING — AMES had until May 17, 2026 to respond to the NOV. Beale Infrastructure paused site work while the contractor formalized a non-potable water source. No penalty finalized as of late May 2026. City of Tucson has refused to provide any water service for the project; the project's permanent water supply will rely on on-site groundwater wells under ADWR permits.

Relevance to data centers

Highlights the convergence of CWA-adjacent stormwater/dust control, municipal water-permit politics, and groundwater extraction permitting at a single hyperscale site. Tucson is the first major U.S. city to formally deny municipal water service to a data center on policy grounds — a precedent that may shift hyperscale projects toward private groundwater wells subject to state withdrawal permits rather than CWA-regulated discharges.

Sources
Contractor working on Project Blue data center issued Notice of Violation (news) · Pima County cites Project Blue contractor for air quality violation — Tucson.com (news) · City says Project Blue must stop using Tucson Water for data center construction — Tucson Sentinel (news) · Project Blue's 2 new Tucson wells could draw up to 31 million gallons of water annually — Tucson Sentinel (news) · Project Blue FAQ — Pima County, AZ (state agency action)
Google LLC (applicant via 'Project Raspberry'), Greenfield Industrial Park, Daleville, Botetourt County, Virginia — three 300,000 sq ft data center buildings on 343.6 acres (2025-2026)Data Center
Section 404 individual permit (Army Corps Norfolk District NAO-2025-01857); Virginia §401 Water Quality Certification from DEQ required; Botetourt County zoning approval independent track
Violation

Not an enforcement action — a §404 individual permit application in active federal review. Google's Project Raspberry data center campus in Botetourt County would impact approximately 3.24 acres of non-tidal wetlands and 6,715 linear feet of stream channels in unnamed tributaries to Tinker Creek and Rulman Branch (Roanoke River basin). Public notice issued March 10, 2026; comment period closed April 9, 2026. Separately, Google litigated through December 2025 to suppress its projected water use under Virginia FOIA; a judge ordered disclosure on November 5, 2025 — the disclosed figure was 8 million gallons per day at full campus buildout.

Outcome

PENDING — Army Corps environmental review ongoing as of June 2026. Virginia §401 WQC not yet issued. Google has offered to purchase wetland mitigation bank credits. The FOIA disclosure of 8 MGD projected water use drew public attention to the scale of data center water demand in the Roanoke Valley water shed.

Relevance to data centers

One of the largest §404 individual permit applications for a single data center campus currently in the federal pipeline in Virginia. The FOIA fight over water-use projections (8 MGD/day at full buildout) is itself precedent-setting: Virginia courts confirmed that data center water-use records held by public utilities are public records, not proprietary business information — a significant transparency ruling for the tracker's data-collection strategy.

Sources

Google LLC (applicant via 'Project Loch'), near Route 288 and Watkins Center Parkway, Chesterfield County, Virginia — three-building data center campus on approximately 350 acres (2026)Data Center
Section 404 individual permit (Army Corps Norfolk District NAO-2026-00182); Virginia §401 Water Quality Certification required
Violation

Not an enforcement action — a §404 individual permit application filed in 2026. Google's Project Loch data center campus in Chesterfield County would impact approximately 4.8 acres of wetlands and 2,828 linear feet of stream channels. Part of Google's announced $9 billion Virginia data center expansion program. Public notice issued May 2026.

Outcome

PENDING — Army Corps environmental review and public comment phase ongoing as of June 2026. Virginia §401 WQC not yet issued.

Relevance to data centers

Together with Project Raspberry (NAO-2025-01857) and the Little Rock AR application, Google's concurrent §404 permit applications in Virginia show that hyperscaler data center construction is generating a wave of individual §404 permits in the federal pipeline — each with multi-acre wetland and multi-thousand-foot stream impacts. The cumulative wetland/stream impact of Virginia's data center build-out is now large enough to warrant cumulative-impact analysis under NEPA.

Sources

Willowbend Capital LLC (Google front company), Port of Little Rock, Pulaski County, Arkansas — $1 billion, 5-building, 1.43 million sq ft data center campus (2026)Data Center
Section 404 individual permit (Army Corps Little Rock District SWL-2026-00038); Arkansas §401 Water Quality Certification from ADEQ required
Violation

Not an enforcement action — the largest §404 wetland-impact permit application for a data center currently in the federal pipeline (as of June 2026). Google's proposed Port of Little Rock campus would fill approximately 16.8 acres of wetlands and more than 6,000 feet of stream channels, the largest single-site wetland footprint of any data center application in the Army Corps public-notice database. Public demonstrations were held outside the Army Corps Little Rock District building. Google is proposing to purchase wetland mitigation bank credits.

Outcome

PENDING — Corps accepting public comments; environmental review ongoing as of June 2026. No permit issued or denied.

Relevance to data centers

The largest single §404 wetland impact in any active data center permit application, illustrating that hyperscale campuses can generate wetland and stream impacts an order of magnitude larger than construction stormwater GPs alone address. The 16.8-acre/6,000-foot footprint is a credible trigger for individual permit review rather than nationwide permits, giving the Corps and state §401 certifying authority substantial leverage over project design.

Sources
Army Corps SWL-2026-00038 public notice — Willowbend Capital / Port of Little Rock (regulatory filing) · Google's $1B Port of Little Rock data center (Arkansas Online) (news) · Little Rock protesters rally over Google data center, citing water and wetland risks (KATV) (news)
xAI Corp. / CTC Property LLC — Colossus supercomputer campus and on-site greywater recycling plant, 3231 Paul R. Lowry Road, Memphis (Shelby County), Tennessee (2025-2026)Data-Center Adjacent
Tennessee state water program — TDEC Division of Water Resources operating permit for the Colossus greywater recycling plant (CWA Section 402 NPDES is state-administered in Tennessee). Important framing: the active federal citizen suit against xAI (NAACP / Earthjustice, notice of intent filed Feb 2026) is a Clean AIR Act case over ~27 unpermitted methane gas turbines — NOT a CWA action. The water dimension here is the permitted-but-paused wastewater-reuse plant and the aquifer-withdrawal governance around it, not a discharge-violation enforcement order.
Violation

xAI's Colossus campus draws cooling water from the Memphis Sand Aquifer, the city's drinking-water source. To offset that draw, xAI committed to an ~$80 million greywater recycling plant (permittee CTC Property LLC) that would treat effluent from the city's T.E. Maxson wastewater treatment plant for industrial cooling, preserving up to 13 million gallons per day of aquifer water. TDEC's Division of Water Resources accepted the application in February 2025 and held public hearings June 25–26, 2025; residents of the predominantly Black 38109 ZIP code raised environmental-justice objections about cumulative pollution burden. On April 9, 2026, xAI paused construction of the recycling plant, saying it was 'prioritizing other more immediate projects' (finishing the Colossus 2 supercomputer first) — leaving cooling demand on the aquifer in the interim.

Outcome

No CWA enforcement action, NOV, or penalty over water as of mid-2026. The greywater plant is permitted but indefinitely paused (announced April 9, 2026); aquifer-protection group Protect Our Aquifer called the pause 'deeply concerning' and 'alarming,' noting the plant 'was not optional — it was a core commitment.' Memphis Mayor Paul Young said he would keep pressing xAI to build it. Separately, the federal Clean Air Act citizen suit over the gas turbines remains active.

Relevance to data centers

The most prominent AI-data-center water fight to date shows the binding regulatory levers are usually NOT a CWA discharge violation: xAI's promised wastewater-reuse plant — which would have cut aquifer draw by up to 13 MGD — was permitted and then shelved, and no CWA mechanism compels it to build. This mirrors the tracker's core thesis: when a data center's operational water sits behind aquifer withdrawals and municipal-sewer arrangements, CWA discharge enforcement has little grip, and the real pressure points are state withdrawal permits, voluntary commitments, and (as the parallel air-permit suit shows) the Clean Air Act.

Sources
State Issues Operating Permit for Colossus Water Plant — Memphis Flyer (news) · 'Alarming:' xAI is 'prioritizing other projects'; Water plant is still planned — Action News 5 (news) · In South Memphis, Elon Musk's Colossus Operated Gas Turbines Without Appropriate Permits — Inside Climate News (news) · Illegal Pollution from Data Center Power Plants Shouldn't Harm Our Communities. We're Suing xAI. — Earthjustice (advocacy / litigation)
Atlas Development, LLC ('Project Sail', ~830-acre, ~$17B hyperscale campus, Coweta County, Georgia; end-user operator undisclosed); Coweta County government; ~17 resident petitioners. (2026)Data-Center Adjacent
No CWA action — a state-court rezoning challenge (declaratory-judgment / certiorari appeal) in Coweta County Superior Court under Georgia land-use law. Plaintiffs invoke the site's status as a Georgia DNR-designated 'Most Significant Groundwater Recharge Area' in the Middle Chattahoochee River basin and allege undisclosed wetlands.
Violation

On May 7, 2026, seventeen residents filed suit asking a judge to void the county's rezoning and permanently block the campus, arguing the approval understated water and power needs, omitted wetlands, and ignored that the parcel sits atop a designated major groundwater-recharge area. A peer-reviewed study cited in the coverage projects a ~$118M decline in residential property values across ~1,215 parcels within 1.25 miles of the site.

Outcome

Pending in Coweta County Superior Court (appeal filed May 7, 2026, within 30 days of the rezoning decision).

Relevance to data centers

A model 'adjacent' case where the only live legal lever over a data center's water risk is local zoning plus a state groundwater-recharge-area designation — the CWA offers no handle on siting a campus over a drinking-water aquifer, so residents must litigate the rezoning itself to reach the water question.

Sources

Quality Technology Services (QTS Realty Trust) data-center construction site, Fayette County, Georgia; Fayette County Water System. (2025-2026)Data-Center Adjacent
No CWA action — a county water-utility metering and billing dispute. One construction meter went un-captured during the county's switch to Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI); the county explicitly declined to fine QTS, characterizing it as a missed read, not unauthorized use.
Violation

Roughly 13–15 million gallons of water used at the QTS site went unbilled over about six months while nearby Annelise Park residents reported low pressure during a Georgia drought. The county issued an ~$147,000 retroactive bill (a single six-month meter read accounting for ~$100,000) after discovering the meter was not being read electronically as assumed. County officials reported finding no evidence of widespread pressure problems after installing monitoring equipment.

Outcome

Resolved on billing — QTS paid the retroactive charges; no fine and no environmental enforcement. The county now meets regularly with QTS to monitor projected demand through the multi-year build.

Relevance to data centers

Shows data-center water accountability failing at the municipal-metering layer: millions of gallons can vanish from billing with no environmental statute, CWA permit, or penalty ever in play — a transparency gap distinct from pollution enforcement but central to the project's mission of tracking real data-center water use.

Sources

TransGas Development Systems (New York-based); Adams Fork Energy Project — natural-gas-fueled ammonia plant and two data center facilities at Twisted Gun Golf Course (Wharncliffe) and Harless Industrial Park (Holden), Mingo County, West Virginia (2025-2026)Data-Center Adjacent
Section 404 (alleged lack of Army Corps dredge-and-fill permit for project requiring stream/wetland disturbance in West Virginia). ESA Section 7 consultation, NEPA review, and NGA pipeline certificate also cited. No active agency enforcement order has been issued — this is a citizen suit alleging §404 violations; the Army Corps has not independently issued a NOV, cease-and-desist, or penalty.
Violation

On December 3, 2025, ten Mingo County residents filed a federal citizen suit in the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of West Virginia, alleging TransGas has begun advancing ground-disturbing activities for the Adams Fork Energy Project — which would power two data center campuses — without obtaining required CWA §404 dredge-and-fill permits, ESA consultation, NEPA review, or FERC gas-pipeline approval. Plaintiffs named the Army Corps Huntington District, U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service, and FERC as defendants for failing to initiate required regulatory reviews of a project affecting streams and wetlands in the Tug Fork watershed.

Outcome

Judge denied plaintiffs' motion for a temporary restraining order and preliminary injunction. Litigation continuing as of June 2026. No Army Corps enforcement action, NOV, or permit denial has been independently issued.

Relevance to data centers

The first documented CWA §404 citizen suit targeting unpermitted stream/wetland disturbance at a data-center power-supply project. The framing — plaintiffs suing both the developer AND the Army Corps for regulatory inaction — is a template for future citizen enforcement where the Corps fails to require permits for data center construction in sensitive watersheds. The TRO denial does not preclude full merits review or injunctive relief after the administrative record is developed.

Sources
Mingo County residents file federal lawsuit against company building power plants, data center in WV (news) · West Virginia residents file lawsuit against ammonia plant / data centre project (QC Intel) (news)
Rowan Digital Infrastructure ('Project Cinco', 440-acre data-center campus, Medina County, Texas; end-user undisclosed); Edwards Aquifer Authority (EAA); local suppliers including Yancey Water Supply. (2025)Data-Center Adjacent
No CWA action — Texas groundwater law: the Edwards Aquifer Authority's statutory pumping cap layered over Texas's common-law 'Rule of Capture,' plus a voluntary water-conservation arrangement. No NPDES discharge permit is involved.
Violation

A 440-acre data-center campus proposed to draw on the heavily stressed Edwards Aquifer during exceptional drought. Rather than a binding permit denial, the response has been negotiated and voluntary: a local water supplier demanded collaborative design and Rowan agreed to fund aquifer-conservation measures (helping shift farmers off flood irrigation), which the EAA framed as 'a reallocation of the existing water rights to these new purposes.' Multiple data centers are reportedly in development in the county.

Outcome

No permit denial; voluntary conservation investment and water-supply negotiation ongoing as of late 2025.

Relevance to data centers

Captures the Texas pattern where the Rule of Capture and a state aquifer authority — not the CWA — govern a data center's water, and mitigation takes the form of voluntary offsets rather than enforceable discharge or withdrawal limits, leaving the public little regulatory leverage over consumption.

Sources
The impact of billion-dollar data centers in water-stressed Medina County (news) · How One Texas County Struck a Deal With Its Data Centers (news) · Rowan Digital to invest in water conservation program in Medina County, Texas (trade press)
Google (Berkeley County data-center campus near Moncks Corner, South Carolina); South Carolina DHEC; Southern Environmental Law Center / Coastal Conservation League. (2016-2019)Data-Center Adjacent
No CWA action — a state groundwater-withdrawal permit under SC DHEC plus a negotiated settlement with SELC/CCL governing the ~83-million-year-old Middendorf Aquifer. Groundwater allocation is state law, not federal CWA discharge regulation.
Violation

Google sought to roughly triple its permitted withdrawal to about 1.5 million gallons per day (~547 million gallons/year) from the Middendorf Aquifer to cool an expanded server farm, even as DHEC was separately asking the town of Mount Pleasant to cut its aquifer use. After public opposition, the December 16, 2019 settlement restricts Google to using groundwater only 'as a last resort when all other water supplies are exhausted,' requires it to develop alternative supplies (surface water from Charleston Water System), and mandates public quarterly use reporting instead of the annual reporting otherwise required by law.

Outcome

Settlement in force since December 2019; Google was steered toward surface water and capped its routine groundwater draw. Subsequent withdrawal upticks have drawn renewed local scrutiny over aquifer depletion.

Relevance to data centers

A flagship example of a negotiated, voluntary-leaning groundwater commitment governed entirely by a state permit and a private settlement — no CWA discharge permit ever bounded the withdrawal — showing how data-center water limits in the Southeast rest on state allocation law and advocacy settlements rather than federal enforcement.

Sources
Groundwater Withdrawal: Google and Southern Environmental Law Center Enter into Agreement Addressing South Carolina Aquifer (legal analysis) · Google agrees to limit groundwater use from S.C. aquifer (advocacy/legal) · Google Groundwater Permit (project page) (advocacy)

New York Data Center Bill Passes Legislature, Awaits Hochul Signature
New York Legislature · 2026-06
The Responsible Data Center Development Act (S10642 / A11560) passed the Senate 44-16 and Assembly 102-39, mandating SEQRA environmental review for any facility exceeding 50 MW. If signed, it would be the broadest state-level environmental review trigger for data centers in the US. Gov. Hochul has not publicly committed as of June 2026.

Bipartisan Michigan Lawmakers Push Data Center Construction Moratorium
Michigan Legislature · 2026-06
SB 1018-1020 and companion HB 5594-5596 would impose a moratorium on new data center construction permits until the state completes a water-capacity analysis and Great Lakes Compact review. A bipartisan Capitol event in June renewed pressure, though Senate leadership prefers targeted regulation over a blanket moratorium.

Illinois Governor Pritzker Pauses New Data Center Incentive Agreements
Illinois Governor&#x27;s Office · 2026-06
After the POWER Act (SB 3830) failed to pass before the session ended, Gov. Pritzker used executive authority to pause new Data Center Investment Program incentive agreements. The move signals growing state reluctance to extend tax incentives without commensurate water and power transparency requirements.
PolicyRegulation
→ IL SB 3830 in Legislation tab
Google Files §404 Individual Permit Applications for Three Data Center Projects
US Army Corps of Engineers · 2026-05
Google filed individual §404 permit applications for Project Raspberry (VA), Project Loch (VA), and a Port of Little Rock (AR) site. The Arkansas application involves 16.8 acres of wetland fill — the largest §404 wetland footprint in any active data center permit. Each application triggers a public comment period and Section 401 water quality certification from the respective state.
Enforcementpermits

Utah Governor Signs Executive Order Requiring Water-Use Review Before Data Center Permits
Utah Governor&#x27;s Office · 2026-05
Gov. Spencer Cox signed EO 2026-03 directing state agencies to require water-use impact review before approving large data center projects. The EO also instructs the Department of Natural Resources to evaluate cost-attribution models so developers bear marginal supply expansion costs. It complements Utah HB 76 (enacted March 2026), which mandates projected and actual water disclosure at a 10,000 sq ft threshold.
RegulationPolicy

Virginia Enacts First Mandatory Data Center Water Reporting Law
Virginia Governor&#x27;s Office · 2026-04
Gov. Spanberger signed HB 496 (companion SB 553) on April 13, 2026, amending Va. Code § 62.1-44.38 to require water utilities to report monthly aggregate water volumes delivered to data centers. The law is effective July 1, 2026; first published data is expected late 2026. It is the first enacted state law mandating utility-level DC water reporting in the US.
RegulationPolicy

Maryland General Assembly · 2026-04
The Maryland General Assembly voted to override Gov. Moore's veto of HB 270 / SB 116, requiring the Department of Natural Resources to conduct an economic and environmental impact study of large data centers before 2028. The study must include water consumption projections and infrastructure cost allocation.
RegulationPolicy

Amazon Pays $20.5M in First Eight-Figure Hyperscaler Water Settlement
Oregon Department of Justice / Oregon DEQ · 2026-03
Amazon and its WWTP operator settled with Oregon DEQ and DOJ over nitrogen discharge at the Boardman industrial park — the first direct hyperscaler water-penalty case to reach eight figures. Data center cooling-tower blowdown was among the industrial loads routed to the facility. The settlement funds nutrient trading credits in the Columbia River basin.
Enforcementsettlements
→ Amazon Boardman OR case in CWA Cases tab — Historical section
Idaho Becomes First State to Mandate Closed-Loop (Non-Consumptive) Cooling for Data Centers
Idaho Legislature · 2026-03
Idaho H895 requires new data center facilities to use closed-loop or non-consumptive cooling systems as a condition of permitting. This eliminates the largest pathway of consumptive water loss — evaporation from open cooling towers. The law is designed to protect groundwater in the Snake River Plain aquifer system.
RegulationSolutions
→ ID H895 in Legislation tab
South Dakota Enacts 'Data Center Bill of Rights' with Water-Supply Compatibility Rule
South Dakota Legislature · 2026-03
SB 135 requires data centers ≥10 MW to demonstrate water-supply compatibility before construction approval, and mandates cost-causation billing so developers bear the marginal cost of supply-infrastructure expansions they trigger — not residential ratepayers. Part of a broader 'bill of rights' package addressing grid and water load-shifting.
RegulationPolicy
→ SD SB 135 in Legislation tab
Utah Enacts Water Transparency Amendments Lowering Disclosure Threshold to 10,000 sq ft
Utah Legislature · 2026-03
Utah HB 76 mandates that data centers ≥10,000 sq ft publicly report projected and actual water consumption annually. The 10,000 sq ft threshold was set to align with existing utility metering capabilities. It is a successor to earlier bills that stalled over data-collection infrastructure concerns.

California Legislature · 2026-05
AB 2619 (Asm. Papan) advances through committee, requiring data centers to publicly disclose projected and actual water use annually. It is the successor to AB 93, which Gov. Newsom vetoed in October 2024 citing data-collection concerns. Companion SB 887 would add CEQA review requirements. Both bills are expected to see floor votes in summer 2026.
RegulationPolicy

Central Ohio Study Projects Data Center Water Demand Growing from 10 to 90 MGD by 2050
Central Ohio Regional Water Authority / Ohio EPA · 2025-03
A regional water demand study published March 2025 projects industrial water demand — dominated by planned data center expansions — growing from ~10 MGD today to 40 MGD by 2030 and 90 MGD by 2050. Intel's New Albany campus alone accounts for ~6 MGD. The study is being used to plan $2B+ in regional water infrastructure.

Ohio EPA · 2025-01
Comment period closed January 2025 on Ohio EPA OHD000001 — the first proposed general permit requiring data centers to file Discharge Monitoring Reports (DMRs) directly for cooling-tower blowdown. Currently, most DC cooling water routes through municipal WWTPs with no facility-level accounting. Final permit is still pending.
RegulationData & Reports

UC Riverside 'Making AI Less Thirsty' — First Major Quantification of AI Water Footprint
University of California, Riverside (Shaolei Ren et al.) · 2023-06
The landmark study estimated 0.5 L per 10–50 ChatGPT queries (direct cooling only) and up to 500 mL per query when including thermoelectric cooling for electricity generation. It catalyzed policy and media attention on data center water use and established WUE (Water Usage Effectiveness) as a public-facing metric. The viral 'one 500mL bottle per prompt' figure in later coverage was a 50–250× overstatement of this research.

Data Center Water Solutions

Solutions to data center water challenges documented across this tracker — organized by who is driving them: state and federal regulators, water utilities, and industry operators. Status badges indicate real-world deployment stage.

16 solutions across 3 categories

Policy & Regulatory Solutions

Enacted laws, permits, and proposed rules that require or incentivize water-responsible data center siting, design, and reporting.

Deployed
Virginia Monthly DC Water Reporting Mandate
State: Virginia General Assembly / Dept. of Environmental Quality · Source
HB 496 / SB 553 (enacted April 2026) requires water utilities to report monthly aggregate volumes delivered to data centers to the State Water Control Board. Creates the first mandatory utility-level data stream for DC water use in the US.
Loudoun Water (serving ~1.635B gal/yr to data centers) must file monthly reports beginning ~July 2026. First published data will be the only facility-aggregated DC water series in any US state.
→ VA HB 496 / SB 553 tracked in Legislation tab
Deployed
Idaho Closed-Loop Cooling Mandate
State: Idaho Legislature
H895 (enacted 2026) requires new data centers to use closed-loop or non-consumptive cooling systems as a permit condition, eliminating the largest evaporative water loss pathway. Designed to protect the Snake River Plain aquifer.
First state-level mandate of its kind in the US. South Carolina HB 4583 (pending) would extend this closed-loop model to the Southeast.
→ ID H895 and SC HB 4583 in Legislation tab
Deployed
South Dakota Cost-Causation Billing
State: South Dakota Legislature
SB 135 (enacted 2026) requires data centers ≥10 MW to demonstrate water-supply compatibility and mandates that developers bear the marginal cost of supply-infrastructure expansions they trigger — not residential ratepayers.
Part of South Dakota's 'Data Center Bill of Rights.' Addresses the pattern where large industrial loads trigger regional water infrastructure upgrades whose costs are socialized to all ratepayers.
→ SD SB 135 in Legislation tab
Deployed
Utah Water Disclosure Law + Executive Order
State: Utah Legislature / Governor Spencer Cox
HB 76 (enacted March 2026) mandates projected and actual water consumption disclosure from facilities ≥10,000 sq ft. EO 2026-03 (May 2026) directs agencies to require water-impact review before approvals and evaluate cost-attribution models.
Disclosure threshold set to match existing utility metering capabilities, making reporting immediately actionable rather than dependent on new infrastructure.
→ UT HB 76 and UT EO 2026-03 in Legislation tab
Pilot / In Progress
Ohio EPA Data Center Wastewater General Permit (OHD000001)
Federal: Ohio EPA (US EPA Region 5) · Source
First proposed general permit requiring data centers to file Discharge Monitoring Reports (DMRs) directly for cooling-tower blowdown, closing the gap where DC cooling water routes through municipal WWTPs with no facility-level accounting.
Comment period closed January 2025; permit finalization pending as of June 2026. If enacted, it would create the first direct federal-level DC water monitoring dataset, complementing the WWTP-level data this project already tracks via EPA ECHO.
→ OHD000001 tracked by oh_epa_general_permit scraper — see Data tab
Proposed
New York SEQRA Environmental Review for 50MW+ Facilities
State: New York Legislature (awaiting governor signature) · Source
S10642 / A11560 requires SEQRA environmental-impact assessment and public comment before any data center project exceeds 50 MW, including a mandatory water-supply analysis. Would be the broadest state environmental-review trigger for data centers in the US.
Passed Senate 44-16 and Assembly 102-39 as of June 2026. Gov. Hochul has not publicly committed to signing or vetoing.
→ NY S10642 / A11560 in Legislation tab

Utility & Infrastructure Solutions

Water utility programs and regional planning approaches that integrate data center demand into supply management, capacity planning, and dual-feed infrastructure.

Deployed
Dual-Feed Systems: Potable + Reclaimed Water Connections
Utility: Loudoun Water (Virginia) · Source
Data centers receive two separate connections — potable for human-contact uses and reclaimed (recycled, non-potable) water for cooling-tower makeup — reducing pressure on drinking water supplies.
Loudoun Water ACFR 2023: 899M gal potable + 736M gal reclaimed delivered to data centers annually. Reclaimed water is 45% of total DC supply — the highest reclaimed fraction of any US data center utility cluster tracked here.
→ Loudoun Water ACFR data tracked in Data tab
Deployed
ERU-Based Demand Planning via Industrial User Surveys
Utility: Prince William Water (Virginia) · Source
Annual Industrial User Surveys allocate Equivalent Residential Units (ERUs) to data center customers, giving utility planners binding demand commitments for capital planning before construction begins.
2024 survey: 56 data centers hold 3,276 ERUs = ~478M gal/yr (avg 58.5 ERU per facility). ERU allocations are reviewed with each expansion permit, creating a cumulative demand ledger that's used to size infrastructure upgrades.
→ Prince William Water IUS data in Data tab
Deployed
Aggregate DC Water Metering and Annual Public Reporting
Utility: Loudoun Water, Fairfax Water (Virginia) · Source
Utilities publish aggregate data center water deliveries in annual financial reports (ACFRs), providing the only publicly available utility-level DC water volumes in Virginia — currently the primary Tier 1 data source for this tracker.
Loudoun Water ACFR 2023: 1.635B gal to data centers (~15% of total utility sales). Fairfax Water supplies ~18 MGD wholesale to Loudoun. Virginia HB 496 will formalize and extend this to monthly reports.
→ Loudoun Water and Fairfax Water data in Data tab
Pilot / In Progress
Regional Demand Forecasting with Data Center Growth Scenarios
Utility: Central Ohio Regional Water Authority / Ohio EPA · Source
Collaborative regional water demand study incorporating data center growth scenarios to enable proactive infrastructure investment rather than reactive expansion after capacity constraints appear.
March 2025 study projects industrial demand: ~10 MGD (2024) → 40 MGD (2030) → 90 MGD (2050). Intel New Albany campus alone: ~6 MGD. Study is informing $2B+ in regional water infrastructure planning.
→ Central Ohio projections in Data tab

Technology & Operational Solutions

Engineering approaches that reduce or eliminate water consumption through improved cooling design, alternative water sources, and efficiency metrics.

Deployed
Closed-Loop Recirculating Cooling Towers
Industry: Hyperscalers (AWS, Google, Microsoft, Meta) — industry standard
Recirculating systems reuse the same water in a closed loop, dramatically reducing freshwater withdrawal compared to once-through systems. This is the current standard for all modern hyperscale facilities and the technology basis for Idaho's closed-loop mandate.
Typical WUE (Water Usage Effectiveness): 1.5–2.0 L/kWh for recirculating towers vs. 3–4 L/kWh for older designs. Industry target: WUE < 1.2 L/kWh. Still consumptive (evaporation) but far more efficient than alternatives.
Deployed
Reclaimed / Recycled Water Sourcing
Industry: Google (Prineville OR), Meta, Loudoun Water DC customers
Data centers contract with utilities or municipalities for reclaimed (non-potable recycled) water to supply cooling towers, freeing potable water for human use. Requires dual-plumbing infrastructure but is well-established in water-stressed regions.
Loudoun Water: 736M gal/yr reclaimed delivered to data centers (45% of total DC supply). Google's Prineville, OR campus uses 100% reclaimed water for cooling. Reclaimed water WUE is identical to potable — the gain is purely in source substitution.
→ Loudoun Water reclaimed volumes in Data tab
Deployed
Water Usage Effectiveness (WUE) Self-Reporting
Industry: Green Grid (standard-setter), Google, Microsoft, Meta, Amazon
WUE (liters of water per kilowatt-hour of IT load) is an industry self-reporting metric enabling year-over-year comparison. Analogous to PUE for power efficiency, it allows tracking progress without facility-level disclosure — though no independent verification exists.
Google 2024: WUE 1.10 L/kWh (global average). Microsoft FY2023: WUE 0.33 L/kWh (includes 'net-water replenishments' — accounting treatment disputed). Meta: 0.26 L/kWh. No independent auditor verifies these figures — see Company Claims tab for verbatim operator quotes.
→ Company Claims in Legislation tab (Company Water Claims section)
Pilot / In Progress
Direct Liquid Cooling (DLC) for AI / GPU Clusters
Industry: Google, Microsoft, Meta (AI training facilities)
Liquid coolant is circulated directly to processors and server components, removing heat without relying on large evaporative cooling towers. Near-zero cooling water required for DLC-equipped racks — the cooling burden shifts to heat exchangers that can reject to ambient air.
Adoption accelerating for AI training clusters (H100/H200/GB200 GPU pods) where heat density exceeds air-side cooling limits. Microsoft reports WUE near 0 for full-DLC racks. At scale, eliminates the cooling-tower evaporation entirely.
Pilot / In Progress
Air-Side / Dry Cooling (Zero Evaporation)
Industry: Microsoft (Dublin, Ireland; Helsinki, Finland), UK operators
In cooler climates, outside air rejects heat without any water evaporation — 'free cooling' or 'economizer mode.' Zero consumptive water use when operating in full air-side mode. Feasibility depends on wet-bulb temperature and local climate.
Microsoft's Dublin data center uses air-side cooling 95%+ of the year, achieving near-zero WUE. Not viable for Virginia summer conditions without backup wet cooling; becomes more viable for data centers sited in northern latitudes or at altitude.
Proposed
Immersion Cooling (Full Liquid Submersion)
Industry: Submer, Wiwynn, GRC, early Microsoft pilots
Servers are fully submerged in dielectric (non-conductive) fluid that absorbs heat directly. The fluid is cooled via compact heat exchangers that can reject to ambient air with minimal or zero evaporative water loss — potentially eliminating cooling water entirely at scale.
Commercial deployments remain rare at hyperscale as of 2026, primarily in HPC and crypto mining. Microsoft and others have published pilot results. Long-term potential to reduce WUE to ~0 without requiring a cold climate or DLC-compatible processor packages.

Dataset last updated 2026-06-24. Deployment status is as of the dataset date; check source links for current status.

Per-query water estimates explainer

Per-Query Water: Why Estimates Vary by 2,000x

How much water does one AI query use?

Estimates range from 0.26 mL to 519 mL per query. The huge range is not a mistake — it reflects fundamentally different accounting methods.

0.26 mL (Google Gemini (self-reported)) 519 mL (UC Riverside (upper bound, with power plant))

Four variables drive the variance:

  1. Inference vs. training — Training a large model is a one-time cost amortized over billions of queries; inference is per-request.
  2. Cooling technology — Evaporative cooling consumes water; air-cooled or liquid-to-liquid systems use much less.
  3. Direct vs. indirect water — On-site cooling is ~20% of total footprint; thermoelectric cooling at power plants is ~80%.
  4. Withdrawal vs. consumption — Withdrawal counts water taken; consumption counts water not returned. Withdrawal numbers are 3-5x higher.

Reality check — per Andy Masley. Including the electricity-generation water, one query is ~2 mL. Translated into everyday terms:

Same water as…= this many AI prompts
Heating a kettle125
PS5 for 1 hour200
One sheet of paper2,550
A warm bath5,000
One American's daily water footprint800,000
Reading a 400-page book1,000,000
Manufacturing a T-shirt1,300,000
Manufacturing a pair of jeans5,400,000
The 500 mL bottle myth. The viral 'one bottle per email/prompt' figure (Washington Post, 2023) was inflated 50–250×. The underlying research actually found ~500 mL per 20–50 prompts — not per single prompt.

Why this tracker measures facilities, not chatbots. Per query is trivial. That's why we track WWTP discharge volumes, utility sales to data centers, and policy mandates — where the aggregate, local impact is real and measurable.

Detailed estimates
  • 0.26 mL — Google Gemini (self-reported)
    Direct on-site cooling only | Source: Google Environmental Report 2024
  • 1.0 mL — Andy Masley estimate
    Direct cooling, calibrated to Google disclosure | Source: Substack analysis, 2024
  • 10 mL — Shaolei Ren / UC Riverside (median)
    Includes server-room cooling | Source: Making AI Less Thirsty (2023)
  • 519 mL — UC Riverside (upper bound, with power plant)
    Includes thermoelectric cooling for electricity generation | Source: Making AI Less Thirsty (2023)

Comparisons from Andy Masley, "The AI water issue is fake" (blog.andymasley.com/p/the-ai-water-issue-is-fake).

Page 71 of 112 Chapter 6 - Sanitary Sewer Inspection, Testing, and Acceptance 6.01 Introduction The following Chapter describes the minimum requirements and general procedures for the inspection and testing of sanitary and storm sewers to be dedicated to Co; City Utilities. Any section of sewer not passing the tests prescribed herein shall be repaired to the satisfaction and approval of Co; Upon completion of installation of the piping and appurtenances, the Co; Work. The Co; Provide post installation sanitary sewer line jet cleaning and television inspection of the sewer line. All video recordings are to be turned over to Co; City Utilities for Engineer review and final approval. Any video deemed incomplete or otherwise deficient shall be re-televised before Co; All lines or sections of lines that are found to be laid improperly with respect to line or grade, that are found to contain broken or leaking sections of pipe, or are obstructed in such a manner that they cannot be satisfactorily corrected otherwise, shall be removed and replaced at the Page 72 of 112 Co; To test for leaks, all completed piping as specified herein after backfilling shall be tested by low-pressure air test, exfiltration, or infiltration test. Should the low pressure air test results be inconclusive, an exfiltration or infiltration test will be required on the low pressure air tested segments. Labor, equipment and supplies required for all tests shall be furnished by the Co; In order to test for infiltration Co; To test for infiltration, Co; City Utilities may also require that the Co; Infiltration tests Page 73 of 112 shall consist of weir measurement to determine quantities of any infiltration. Measurements shall be taken at line locations determined on-site. This infiltration test will not be made until the sewer line is completed, and the Co; Contractor at his own expense. Equipment and supplies required from smoke tests shall be furnished by the Co; Other supplementary smoke tests prior to backfilling may be performed by the Co; Contractor and observed by Co; All pipe, fittings, valves, and other materials found to be defective under test shall be removed and replaced at no additional expense to Co; City Utilities. f. Lines which fail to meet tests shall be repaired and retested as necessary until test requirements are complied with. g. All water for testing shall be metered and cost of water shall be paid for by Co; Engineer. c. Co; City Utilities shall witness all tests. The procedure for testing shall be approved by Co; Before testing, heat fusion joints shall be completely cooled. Mechanical joints shall be completely assembled. Co; Testing may be conducted on the entire system or sections of the system. Test section length shall be determined by the Co; The pipeline shall be re-tested after a minimum 8-hour recuperation period. All water for testing shall be metered and cost of water shall be paid for by Co; C. Sanitary Sewer Manholes 1. Testing Prior to Backfilling Page 78 of 112 a. This specification shall govern the vacuum testing of sanitary sewer manholes and structures and shall be used as a method of determining acceptability by Co; City Utilities, in accepting maintenance of a sanitary sewer manhole or structure on behalf of the public. This test shall be performed in accordance with ASTM C 1244 prior to backfilling. Other forms of testing of some manholes may be required, as deemed necessary by Co; All temporary plugs and braces shall be removed after each test. Manholes will be accepted as having passed the vacuum test requirements if they meet the criteria stated above. Page 65 of 112 Chapter 5 - Water Installation 5.01 Introduction The following Chapter addresses the minimum requirements for the installation of water distribution facilities. 5.02 Hydrant Installation A. Valves 1. Location a. Valves shall be spaced no greater than 600 feet apart on water mains 12-inches and smaller. b. Valves shall be spaced no greater than 1,200 feet apart on water mains 16-inches and larger. B. Fire Hydrants 1. Location a. Fire hydrants shall be spaced no greater than 500 feet apart. b. Hydrants shall be located as shown or as directed by Co; City Utilities so as to provide complete accessibility and minimize the possibility of damage from vehicles or injury to pedestrians. c. When placed behind the curb, the hydrant barrel shall be set so that the pumper or hose nozzle cap will be a minimum of 18-inches and a maximum of 24-inches from the back of curb in developed areas. d. When placed behind the edge of pavement, the hydrant barrel shall be set so that the pumper or hose nozzle cap will be a minimum of 3-feet and a maximum of 5- feet from the edge of pavement in undeveloped areas. Deviations from these standards will only be allowed at Co; Hydrants shall be located as shown on the Co; Drawings or as directed by Co; Details in the Drawings, or as directed by Co; Yard hydrants located on sanitary sewer lift station sites are not required to be metered. Page 65 of 112 Chapter 5 - Water Installation 5.01 Introduction The following Chapter addresses the minimum requirements for the installation of water distribution facilities. 5.02 Hydrant Installation A. Valves 1. Location a. Valves shall be spaced no greater than 600 feet apart on water mains 12-inches and smaller. b. Valves shall be spaced no greater than 1,200 feet apart on water mains 16-inches and larger. B. Fire Hydrants 1. Location a. Fire hydrants shall be spaced no greater than 500 feet apart. b. Hydrants shall be located as shown or as directed by Co; City Utilities so as to provide complete accessibility and minimize the possibility of damage from vehicles or injury to pedestrians. c. When placed behind the curb, the hydrant barrel shall be set so that the pumper or hose nozzle cap will be a minimum of 18-inches and a maximum of 24-inches from the back of curb in developed areas. d. When placed behind the edge of pavement, the hydrant barrel shall be set so that the pumper or hose nozzle cap will be a minimum of 3-feet and a maximum of 5- feet from the edge of pavement in undeveloped areas. Deviations from these standards will only be allowed at Co; Hydrants shall be located as shown on the Co; Drawings or as directed by Co; Details in the Drawings, or as directed by Co; Yard hydrants located on sanitary sewer lift station sites are not required to be metered.
